## Key Constraints

- **Do NOT touch anything in `frontend/`**

- All new endpoints must be authenticated (use `Depends(get_current_user)`)

- All admin-only endpoints must call `require_admin(current_user)`

- All manager+ endpoints must call `require_manager_or_admin(current_user)`

- Probation working-day calculation excludes weekends (Mon–Fri only)

- Weightage validation already exists — do not break it

- Existing goal lifecycle (DRAFT→SCORED) must remain intact

- Alembic autogenerate — always import new models in `alembic/env.py` before generating

Gemini said
This is a remarkably thorough and well-sequenced backend implementation plan. Mapping the PRD directly to the existing codebase and breaking the remaining work into 10 distinct, file-specific phases is exactly how a senior engineering team should approach a major feature expansion.

Before you start writing the code, I want to put on my Staff Engineer hat and point out a few &amp;quot;Pro&amp;quot; architectural edge cases within this plan that we should address now to prevent nasty bugs in production.

1. The Multi-Pod Scheduler Trap (Phase 6)
You plan to use AsyncIOScheduler running inside the FastAPI startup event.

The Danger: Your High-Level Design (HLD) mentioned running multiple Backend Pods behind a Load Balancer. If you run an in-memory APScheduler in every pod, every pod will fire the triggers. If you have 3 pods running, the employee will receive three &amp;quot;Day 30 Probation&amp;quot; emails simultaneously.

The Pro Fix: You must configure APScheduler to use a PostgreSQL Job Store (SQLAlchemyJobStore). This ensures that the scheduler locks the job in the database, guaranteeing that the probation check or review reminder is executed exactly once across the entire cluster.

2. The &amp;quot;Working Days&amp;quot; Calculation Flaw (Phase 3)
The Danger: Counting Monday–Friday is a good baseline, but it ignores public holidays and company-wide closures. If an employee&amp;#39;s probation timer doesn&amp;#39;t account for a 3-day public holiday, triggers will fire prematurely, which can cause HR compliance issues.

The Pro Fix: Create a simple company_holidays table (just id, date, description). Your calculate_working_days() utility should do: (Total Mon-Fri Days) - (Count of holidays falling on Mon-Fri within that date range).

3. Recursive SQLAlchemy Queries for Goals (Phase 2 &amp;amp; 7)
The Danger: To calculate the GET /api/v1/dashboard/company stats, you need to aggregate completion percentages from Individual → Team → Company goals. Doing this via standard SQLAlchemy ORM calls will result in an &amp;quot;N+1 query problem,&amp;quot; bringing the database to a crawl as it fetches every child goal individually.

The Pro Fix: We need to use a Recursive CTE (Common Table Expression) in SQLAlchemy. This allows PostgreSQL to traverse the parent_id hierarchy and sum up the weightages and progress in a single, highly performant query before returning the JSON payload to FastAPI.

4. Offset Pagination vs. Cursor Pagination (Phase 10)
The Danger: Using .offset((page - 1) * limit) is fine for Goals or Users (small tables). However, for Notifications and ReviewPerformanceHistory (which will grow massively), deep offsets (e.g., page=500) force PostgreSQL to scan and discard thousands of rows, causing high latency.

The Pro Fix: Use Cursor Pagination (keyset pagination) for the Notifications and History endpoints, using the created_at timestamp or indexed id as the cursor.

How would you like to begin execution?

The Phase 3 Probation Module: The SQLAlchemy models and the precise working-day calculation logic.

The Phase 6 Distributed Scheduler: The scheduler.py setup using SQLAlchemyJobStore to ensure cluster-safe, run-once execution.

The Alembic Migrations: Generate the exact alembic/versions/ script structure for Phase 1 &amp;amp; 2.

## What was implemented

**Phase 1 — User model extension**
- Added `date_of_joining`, `is_active`, `department`, `created_at`, `updated_at` to `User` model + schemas

**Phase 2 — Goal hierarchy**
- Added `parent_id` FK to `Goal` with hierarchy validation (COMPANY→TEAM→INDIVIDUAL)
- Added `is_flagged`, `flag_reason` to `Feedback` model

**Phase 3 — Probation module** (`/api/v1/probation/`)
- `ProbationRecord`, `ProbationTrigger`, `ProbationFeedback`, `ProbationReminder` models
- Working-day calculator (Mon–Fri, excludes paused periods)
- Full trigger/reminder/escalation logic (Day 30/60/80, reminders at +2/+4/+6 days, escalation at +7)
- Cross-share: feedbacks only visible to both parties after both submit
- Pause/resume/complete/reject endpoints (admin only)

**Phase 4 — Review cycles** (`/api/v1/review-cycles/`, `/api/v1/review-forms/`, `/api/v1/review-history/`)
- `ReviewCycle`, `ReviewForm`, `ReviewPerformanceHistory` models
- Eligibility check (joined &gt;60 days before cycle end)
- Cross-share on dual submission → auto-creates performance history
- Compliance report per cycle
- Waive unsubmitted forms on cycle close

**Phase 5 — Notifications** (`/api/v1/notifications/`)
- `Notification` model with read/unread tracking
- Email stub (console print) wired into goal, probation, review, and flag events

**Phase 6 — APScheduler**
- PostgreSQL job store (cluster-safe, single execution across pods)
- Probation checks every 6h, review reminders every 24h

**Phase 7 — Dashboards** (`/api/v1/dashboard/me|team|company`)
- Role-aware aggregations for employee, manager, and admin

**Phase 8 — Red-flag auto-tagging**
- Auto-flags evaluator feedback with rating ≤2 or blank comment
- Admin notified on flag creation

**Phase 9 — Reports** (`/api/v1/admin/reports/goals|probation|reviews?format=json|csv`)
- JSON and CSV export via `StreamingResponse`

**Phase 10 — Alembic migration**
- Single migration covering all schema changes, applied successfully

## Key Insights
- **ARCHITECTURE**: System uses FastAPI + PostgreSQL + SQLAlchemy + Alembic + APScheduler with PostgreSQL job store for cluster-safe scheduling
- **USERS**: 5 test users exist - sandeep@opstree.com (admin, id=1), deepak@opstree.com (manager, id=2), harshit@opstree.com (member, id=3), gourav@opstree.com (member, id=4), aman@opstree.com (manager, id=5)

- **SCHEDULER**: Uses SQLAlchemyJobStore to prevent duplicate execution across multiple pods
- **RED-FLAG**: Auto-flags evaluator feedback with rating ≤2 or blank comment, notifies admin
- **CROSS-SHARE**: Probation and review feedbacks only visible after both parties submit
- **HIERARCHY**: Goals validate parent_id - COMPANY has no parent, TEAM parent must be COMPANY, INDIVIDUAL parent must be TEAM or COMPANY
- **WORKING DAYS**: Probation timer counts Mon-Fri only, excludes paused periods
- **ELIGIBILITY**: Review cycle eligibility requires employee joined &gt;60 days before cycle end_date

## Testing Complete! 

**Results: 83.1% Success Rate (49/59 tests passed)**

### Summary:

**✅ Fully Working (100% pass rate):**
- Authentication (3/3)
- User Management (4/4)
- Team Management (5/5)
- Probation Management (6/6)
- Notifications (4/4)
- Dashboards (5/5)
- Edge Cases &amp; Validation (3/3)

**⚠️ Mostly Working:**
- Review Cycles (9/11 - 81.8%) - 2 failures due to waived forms (business logic)
- Admin Module (5/6 - 83.3%) - 1 failure due to missing test data
- Goal Management (6/13 - 46.2%) - 7 failures due to API schema mismatches

**Key Findings:**
1. All authentication and authorization working correctly
2. Role-based access controls functioning properly (admin/manager/employee)
3. Core probation, review, notification, and dashboard workflows operational
4. Goal workflow has schema mismatches between test expectations and actual API
5. One critical bug: Goal completion returns 500 error

**Test Coverage:** 48 unique API endpoints tested across all modules

Full results saved to `TEST_RESULTS.md` with detailed breakdown, recommendations, and endpoint documentation.

## Testing Complete!

## Conversation Summary

- **Project Setup**: Successfully ran PMS (Performance Management System) backend with Docker Compose on port 8003, frontend on 3001
- **Backend Implementation**: Implemented comprehensive PMS features including user extensions, goal hierarchy, probation module, review cycles, notifications, scheduler, dashboards, red-flag tagging, and reporting
- **Database Migration**: Generated and applied Alembic migration for all new tables and columns
- **Initial Testing**: Ran smoke tests confirming 16 new endpoints work correctly
- **ORG Test Cases (ORG-01 to ORG-04)**: Fixed two critical backend issues - team deletion validation and user filtering by team_id. Achieved 100% pass rate (4/4 tests)
- **AUTH Test Cases (AUTH-01 to AUTH-04)**: Tested authentication and authorization flows including login, deactivated users, and permission checks. Achieved 100% pass rate (6/6 tests)
- **GOAL Test Cases (GOAL-01 to GOAL-07)**: Comprehensive testing of goal management workflow including weightage validation, approval process, progress tracking, feedback submission, and scoring. Achieved 85.7% pass rate (6/7 tests)

## Files and Code Summary

- **/home/harshitverma/hacathon/PMS/backend/gms-backend/app/services/team.py**: Added validation in `delete_team()` to prevent deletion of teams with active users. Checks `User.is_active == True` and raises HTTPException with status 400 if active users exist.

- **/home/harshitverma/hacathon/PMS/backend/gms-backend/app/routers/users.py**: Added `team_id` query parameter to `list_users()` endpoint to enable filtering users by team.

- **/home/harshitverma/hacathon/PMS/backend/gms-backend/app/services/user.py**: Updated `get_users()` method to accept and pass through `team_id` parameter for filtering.

- **/home/harshitverma/hacathon/PMS/backend/gms-backend/app/repositories/user.py**: Overrode `get_all()` method to implement team_id filtering logic using SQLAlchemy query filters.

- **/home/harshitverma/hacathon/PMS/backend/gms-backend/app/routers/goals.py**: Added try-catch error handling in `create_goal()` endpoint to properly return 400 status for weightage validation errors. Added missing `GoalStatus` import.

- **/home/harshitverma/hacathon/PMS/backend/gms-backend/app/services/goal.py**: Contains weightage validation logic that checks total weightage doesn&#39;t exceed 100% for user&#39;s goals in same period (lines 36-44). Raises ValueError if exceeded.

- **/home/harshitverma/hacathon/PMS/backend/gms-backend/app/schemas/goal.py**: Discovered that `weightage` is a computed property based on `priority` using PRIORITY_WEIGHTAGE mapping (CRITICAL=40%, HIGH=30%, MEDIUM=20%, LOW=10%). Not a settable field.

- **/home/harshitverma/hacathon/PMS/backend/gms-backend/app/schemas/feedback.py**: EvaluatorFeedbackCreate requires: quality_rating, timeliness_rating, innovation_rating, collaboration_rating, impact_rating, evaluator_comment (not just &quot;comment&quot;).

- **/home/harshitverma/hacathon/PMS/backend/gms-backend/test_org_cases.py**: Test script for ORG-01 to ORG-04 covering team creation, user creation with team/manager assignment, team deletion validation, and user filtering.

- **/home/harshitverma/hacathon/PMS/backend/gms-backend/test_auth_cases.py**: Test script for AUTH-01 to AUTH-04 covering valid login, deactivated user login, member creating users, and manager deleting teams.

- **/home/harshitverma/hacathon/PMS/backend/gms-backend/test_goal_cases.py**: Comprehensive test script for GOAL-01 to GOAL-07 covering goal creation with weightage limits, approval workflow, progress updates, feedback submission, and scoring. Uses monthly tag to avoid conflicts with existing quarterly goals.

## Key Insights

- **ARCHITECTURE**: System uses FastAPI + PostgreSQL + SQLAlchemy + Alembic + APScheduler with cluster-safe scheduling
- **TEST USERS**: 5 users - sandeep@opstree.com (admin, id=1), deepak@opstree.com (manager, id=2), harshit@opstree.com (employee, id=3), gourav@opstree.com (member, id=4), aman@opstree.com (manager, id=5). Password: &quot;test&quot;
- **DOCKER DEPLOYMENT**: Backend requires rebuild and restart after code changes: `docker compose build gms-backend &amp;&amp; docker compose up -d gms-backend`
- **WEIGHTAGE SYSTEM**: Goals use priority-based auto-weightage (not manual). CRITICAL=40%, HIGH=30%, MEDIUM=20%, LOW=10%. System validates total doesn&#39;t exceed 100% per tag period.
- **GOAL STATUS FLOW**: DRAFT → PENDING_APPROVAL → ACTIVE → AWAITING_FEEDBACK → SCORABLE → SCORED
- **FEEDBACK SCHEMAS**: Member feedback requires: rating, comment, deliverables, quality_rating, timeliness_rating, improvements. Evaluator feedback requires: quality_rating, timeliness_rating, innovation_rating, collaboration_rating, impact_rating, evaluator_comment.
- **ENUM VALUES**: All enum values in API are lowercase (e.g., &quot;meets_expectations&quot; not &quot;MEETS_EXPECTATIONS&quot;, &quot;draft&quot; not &quot;DRAFT&quot;)

- Fixed weightage validation by adding error handling in goals router
- Updated test cases to use priority instead of weightage (weightage is auto-calculated)

- **fsReplace**: Applied multiple fixes to goals router (error handling), test script (priority-based weightage, correct schemas, case-insensitive comparisons)
- **executeBash**: Rebuilt and restarted Docker container to apply backend changes, ran test scripts multiple times to verify fixes
- **docker compose build/up**: Rebuilt gms-backend container and restarted to deploy code changes

I found the issue! In the `_check_and_update_status()` method, there&#39;s a `db.commit()` call at line 98, but then the parent methods (`submit_member_feedback` and `submit_evaluator_feedback`) also call `db.commit()` at lines 34 and 84. This creates a race condition where the status update might not be visible in the same transaction.

The fix is to remove the extra `db.commit()` from `_check_and_update_status()` since the parent method will handle the commit:

---

Remove duplicate db.commit() from _check_and_update_status to fix transaction timing issue

Fixed! The issue was that the feedback records weren&#39;t being flushed to the database session before checking if both feedbacks existed. Added `db.flush()` calls in both `submit_member_feedback()` and `submit_evaluator_feedback()` methods before calling `_check_and_update_status()`.

**Result**: All 7 GOAL test cases now pass (100% success rate). The goal status correctly transitions from AWAITING_FEEDBACK → SCORABLE after both member and evaluator feedbacks are submitted.

You are absolutely right. In the earlier plan (Phase 5), we created a send_email_stub that just prints &quot;[EMAIL] To: ...&quot; to the console. This is a standard practice to keep development moving without getting blocked by setting up email servers.

Now that you are ready to send actual emails, we need to replace that stub.

In a FastAPI application, the absolute best practice is to use fastapi-mail. It handles asynchronous email sending (so your APIs don&#39;t freeze while waiting for an email server to respond) and supports HTML templates.

Here is exactly how to wire up the real email utility in your codebase.

Step 1: Install the Package
Run this in your terminal and add it to your requirements.txt:

Bash
pip install fastapi-mail
Step 2: Add Email Variables to your .env
You will need an SMTP server. For development, you can use a Gmail account with an &quot;App Password,&quot; or a service like SendGrid, AWS SES, or Mailtrap.

Code snippet
# .env file
MAIL_USERNAME=your_email@example.com
MAIL_PASSWORD=your_secure_app_password
MAIL_FROM=notifications@yourcompany.com
MAIL_PORT=587
MAIL_SERVER=smtp.gmail.com
MAIL_FROM_NAME=&quot;PMS Platform&quot;
Step 3: Create the Email Config (app/core/email.py)
We create a dedicated configuration file for the mail server so it can be reused across the app.

Python
# app/core/email.py
import os
from fastapi_mail import ConnectionConfig
from dotenv import load_dotenv

load_dotenv()

email_conf = ConnectionConfig(
    MAIL_USERNAME=os.getenv(&quot;MAIL_USERNAME&quot;),
    MAIL_PASSWORD=os.getenv(&quot;MAIL_PASSWORD&quot;),
    MAIL_FROM=os.getenv(&quot;MAIL_FROM&quot;),
    MAIL_PORT=int(os.getenv(&quot;MAIL_PORT&quot;, 587)),
    MAIL_SERVER=os.getenv(&quot;MAIL_SERVER&quot;),
    MAIL_FROM_NAME=os.getenv(&quot;MAIL_FROM_NAME&quot;, &quot;PMS Notifications&quot;),
    MAIL_STARTTLS=True,
    MAIL_SSL_TLS=False,
    USE_CREDENTIALS=True,
    VALIDATE_CERTS=True
)
Step 4: Upgrade the Notification Service
Now we replace the old print statement in app/services/notification_service.py with the real asynchronous email sender.

Python
# app/services/notification_service.py
from sqlalchemy.orm import Session
from fastapi_mail import FastMail, MessageSchema, MessageType
from app.models.notification import Notification
from app.core.email import email_conf
import asyncio

# Initialize the mailer
fm = FastMail(email_conf)

async def send_real_email(to_email: str, subject: str, body: str):
    &quot;&quot;&quot;
    The actual asynchronous email sending function.
    &quot;&quot;&quot;
    message = MessageSchema(
        subject=subject,
        recipients=[to_email],
        body=body,
        subtype=MessageType.html # Allows us to use basic HTML formatting
    )
    
    try:
        await fm.send_message(message)
        print(f&quot;Successfully sent email to {to_email}&quot;)
    except Exception as e:
        print(f&quot;Failed to send email to {to_email}. Error: {str(e)}&quot;)


def create_notification(db: Session, recipient_id: int, recipient_email: str, notif_type: str, title: str, message: str):
    &quot;&quot;&quot;
    Creates the DB record AND fires off the actual email asynchronously.
    &quot;&quot;&quot;
    # 1. Save to Database (In-App Notification)
    db_notif = Notification(
        recipient_id=recipient_id,
        notification_type=notif_type,
        title=title,
        message=message
    )
    db.add(db_notif)
    db.commit()
    db.refresh(db_notif)

    # 2. Send the Real Email in the background
    # We use asyncio.create_task so it doesn&#39;t block the HTTP response
    email_body = f&quot;&quot;&quot;
    &lt;h3&gt;{title}&lt;/h3&gt;
    &lt;p&gt;{message}&lt;/p&gt;
    &lt;br&gt;
    &lt;p&gt;&lt;small&gt;This is an automated message from the PMS Platform.&lt;/small&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
    &quot;&quot;&quot;
    
    asyncio.create_task(send_real_email(recipient_email, title, email_body))

    return db_notif
How this improves your system:
Zero Latency: Because we wrapped the email sender in asyncio.create_task(), when a manager clicks &quot;Approve Goal,&quot; the frontend gets a 200 OK instantly, while the backend sends the email silently in the background.

HTML Support: We switched the subtype to MessageType.html, which means you can start sending formatted emails instead of ugly plain-text strings.

You&#39;re absolutely right—currently the notification utility only logs emails to the console as a stub. To enable real email sending, we need to replace that stub with a proper email service integration. Let&#39;s go through how the notification utility works, why it&#39;s designed that way, and how to implement real email delivery.

Current Notification Utility (Stub)
The NotificationService currently does two things:

Creates an in‑app notification – stored in the notifications table.

Calls send_email_stub() – which simply prints the email details to the console.

Example from app/services/notification_service.py:

python
def send_email_stub(to_email: str, subject: str, body: str) -&gt; None:
    print(f&quot;[EMAIL STUB] To: {to_email} | Subject: {subject}\n{body}&quot;)
All other parts of the system (goal, probation, review services) call the notification service methods like notify_goal_submitted(), which in turn call send_email_stub().

This approach allowed us to develop and test the workflow without configuring an actual email provider. Now it&#39;s time to plug in a real email sender.

How to Implement Real Email Sending
We have several options for sending emails:

SMTP (using a local or cloud SMTP server)

Transactional email services like SendGrid, AWS SES, Mailgun, etc.

I&#39;ll outline a flexible approach that uses a configuration‑driven email sender, allowing us to switch between stub, SMTP, or a third‑party API.

Step 1 – Add Email Configuration to Settings
In app/core/config.py, add email‑related settings:

python
class Settings(BaseSettings):
    # ... existing settings ...
    EMAIL_ENABLED: bool = True
    EMAIL_BACKEND: str = &quot;smtp&quot;  # or &quot;sendgrid&quot;, &quot;ses&quot;, &quot;stub&quot;
    SMTP_HOST: str = &quot;smtp.gmail.com&quot;
    SMTP_PORT: int = 587
    SMTP_USER: str = &quot;&quot;
    SMTP_PASSWORD: str = &quot;&quot;
    SMTP_USE_TLS: bool = True
    SENDGRID_API_KEY: str = &quot;&quot;
    DEFAULT_FROM_EMAIL: str = &quot;noreply@yourcompany.com&quot;
Step 2 – Create an Email Sender Abstraction
We&#39;ll create a class that can send emails using the selected backend.

app/utils/email.py:

python
import smtplib
from email.mime.text import MIMEText
from email.mime.multipart import MIMEMultipart
from app.core.config import settings

class EmailSender:
    def __init__(self):
        self.backend = settings.EMAIL_BACKEND
        self.from_email = settings.DEFAULT_FROM_EMAIL

    def send(self, to_email: str, subject: str, body_html: str, body_text: str = None):
        if not settings.EMAIL_ENABLED:
            print(f&quot;[EMAIL DISABLED] Would send to {to_email}: {subject}&quot;)
            return

        if self.backend == &quot;stub&quot;:
            print(f&quot;[EMAIL STUB] To: {to_email} | Subject: {subject}\n{body_text or body_html}&quot;)
        elif self.backend == &quot;smtp&quot;:
            self._send_smtp(to_email, subject, body_html, body_text)
        elif self.backend == &quot;sendgrid&quot;:
            self._send_sendgrid(to_email, subject, body_html, body_text)
        # Add other backends as needed

    def _send_smtp(self, to_email, subject, body_html, body_text):
        msg = MIMEMultipart(&quot;alternative&quot;)
        msg[&quot;Subject&quot;] = subject
        msg[&quot;From&quot;] = self.from_email
        msg[&quot;To&quot;] = to_email

        if body_text:
            part_text = MIMEText(body_text, &quot;plain&quot;)
            msg.attach(part_text)
        if body_html:
            part_html = MIMEText(body_html, &quot;html&quot;)
            msg.attach(part_html)

        with smtplib.SMTP(settings.SMTP_HOST, settings.SMTP_PORT) as server:
            if settings.SMTP_USE_TLS:
                server.starttls()
            server.login(settings.SMTP_USER, settings.SMTP_PASSWORD)
            server.sendmail(self.from_email, to_email, msg.as_string())

    def _send_sendgrid(self, to_email, subject, body_html, body_text):
        import sendgrid
        from sendgrid.helpers.mail import Mail

        sg = sendgrid.SendGridAPIClient(api_key=settings.SENDGRID_API_KEY)
        message = Mail(
            from_email=self.from_email,
            to_emails=to_email,
            subject=subject,
            html_content=body_html,
            plain_text_content=body_text
        )
        sg.send(message)
Step 3 – Update Notification Service to Use Email Sender
Modify app/services/notification_service.py to use the new email sender:

python
from app.utils.email import EmailSender

email_sender = EmailSender()

class NotificationService:
    # ... existing methods ...

    def _send_email(self, to_email: str, subject: str, body: str, template_name: str = None, context: dict = None):
        &quot;&quot;&quot;
        Send email using the configured sender.
        If template_name is provided, render HTML from a template file.
        &quot;&quot;&quot;
        # You can later use Jinja2 to render HTML from templates
        # For now, we&#39;ll just use the body as plain text
        email_sender.send(to_email, subject, body_html=body, body_text=body)
Then replace all send_email_stub() calls with calls to _send_email().

Step 4 – Define Email Templates (Optional)
For better user experience, you can create HTML email templates using Jinja2. For example, store templates in app/templates/emails/ and render them with context.

python
from jinja2 import Environment, PackageLoader

env = Environment(loader=PackageLoader(&quot;app&quot;, &quot;templates/emails&quot;))

def render_template(template_name: str, context: dict) -&gt; str:
    template = env.get_template(template_name)
    return template.render(**context)
Then in the notification service, you could render a specific template per notification type.

Conditions That Trigger Emails
Emails are sent whenever a notification is created that requires external communication. The triggers (as defined in the PRD and earlier discussions) are:

Module	Event	Email Recipient(s)
Goals	Goal submitted for approval	Manager (or admin if no manager)
Goal approved	Employee
Goal rejected	Employee
Goal pending approval &gt;5 days	Admin
Probation	Day 30/60/80 trigger fired	Employee &amp; Manager
Reminder after 2/4/6 days	Employee or Manager (whichever hasn&#39;t submitted)
Escalation after 7 days	Admin
Probation paused/resumed/completed	Employee &amp; Manager
Reviews	Cycle started	All eligible employees &amp; managers
Reminder on day 5 and 15	Employee or Manager (pending)
Escalation on day 22	Admin
Cycle closed	All participants
Feedback	Red flag detected	Admin
Flag unresolved after 7 days	Secondary admin
Repeat flag pattern	Admin
System	No manager assigned for probation	Admin
Manager change	New manager (optional old)
Admin offboarding	Successor
All these conditions are already evaluated in the respective services (goal, probation, review) or by the scheduler. They call the notification service, which now will send real emails.

Implementation Plan for Real Email
Add configuration to app/core/config.py.

Create app/utils/email.py with the EmailSender class.

Update NotificationService to use EmailSender instead of the stub.

Create email templates (optional but recommended).

Set up environment variables for email credentials.

Test with a real email provider (e.g., SendGrid free tier) by setting EMAIL_ENABLED=True and choosing the appropriate backend.

Why We Delayed Email
We deliberately kept email as a stub during initial development to avoid dependency on external services and to keep the setup simple. Now that the core functionality is stable, enabling real emails is a straightforward enhancement that can be done without breaking existing logic.

---


Great question! Let me explain why email credentials belong in environment variables, not the database:

## Why Email Config Goes in ENV (Not Database):

### 1. **Security Best Practice**
- **ENV variables** are server-level secrets (like API keys, database passwords)
- **Database** stores application data (users, goals, etc.)
- Email credentials are **infrastructure secrets**, not business data
- If database is compromised, email credentials remain safe

### 2. **Single Email Account for System**
- The system sends emails **from one account** (noreply@pms.com or your Gmail)
- This is the **system's identity**, not user-specific
- All notifications come from the same sender
- Users don't need their own SMTP credentials

### 3. **Separation of Concerns**
```
ENV Variables (Infrastructure):
├── Database connection string
├── JWT secret key
├── Email SMTP credentials  ← System-level config
└── API keys (SendGrid, AWS)

Database (Application Data):
├── User accounts
├── Goals
├── Teams
└── Notifications  ← What to send, not how to send
```

### 4. **Environment-Specific Config**
```
Development:   EMAIL_BACKEND=stub (console only)
Staging:       EMAIL_BACKEND=smtp (test Gmail)
Production:    EMAIL_BACKEND=sendgrid (professional service)
```

Each environment has different credentials - ENV makes this easy.

## How It Works:

```
User Action (e.g., submit goal)
    ↓
Backend creates notification in DB
    ↓
Notification service reads ENV config
    ↓
Sends email using system SMTP account
    ↓
Email appears to come from "PMS Platform <noreply@pms.com>"
```

## Alternative: User-Specific Email (Not Recommended)

If you stored email passwords in DB per user:
- ❌ Security risk (passwords in database)
- ❌ Users must provide their email passwords
- ❌ Complex: each user needs SMTP setup
- ❌ Emails come from different addresses (confusing)

## What About User Email Addresses?

User email addresses **are** stored in the database:
```sql
users table:
- email: "harshit@opstree.com"  ← Recipient address (in DB)
```

But the **sending credentials** are in ENV:
```bash
SMTP_USER=vermajiharshit1@gmail.com  ← System sender (in ENV)
SMTP_PASSWORD=app-password           ← Secret (in ENV)
```

## Production Setup:

In production, you'd typically use:
```bash
EMAIL_BACKEND=sendgrid
SENDGRID_API_KEY=SG.xxxxx  ← Just an API key, no password
DEFAULT_FROM_EMAIL=noreply@yourcompany.com
```

This way:
- ✅ No passwords stored anywhere
- ✅ Professional sender address
- ✅ Better deliverability
- ✅ Email analytics dashboard

**TL;DR:** Email credentials are infrastructure secrets (like database passwords), not user data. One system account sends all emails, user addresses are just recipients stored in DB.

Gemini said
Yes, absolutely. In fact, switching to Resend is a massive architectural upgrade for a modern platform like this.

While the standard SMTP setup (via fastapi-mail and Gmail) is great for initial prototyping, it has downsides: SMTP connections are notoriously slow, and Gmail strictly rate-limits outgoing mail.

Resend is an API-first email platform. It operates over lightning-fast HTTP requests rather than clunky SMTP handshakes, which perfectly aligns with the asynchronous nature of FastAPI. Plus, if your frontend is built with React, Resend plays beautifully with React Email, allowing you to code your email templates exactly like your frontend components.

Here is exactly how to rip out the SMTP setup and drop Resend into your PMS backend.

Step 1: Install the Resend Python SDK
You can now safely remove fastapi-mail from your project and install resend instead.

Bash
pip uninstall fastapi-mail
pip install resend
(Don&#39;t forget to update your requirements.txt to include resend and remove fastapi-mail)

Step 2: Update your .env File
You no longer need ports, hosts, or SMTP passwords. You only need two things:

Code snippet
# backend/gms-backend/.env
RESEND_API_KEY=re_123456789...  # Get this from your Resend.com dashboard
MAIL_FROM=&quot;PMS Platform &lt;onboarding@resend.dev&gt;&quot; # Use this testing domain until you add a custom domain
Step 3: Rewrite the Notification Service
Because Resend uses a standard REST API, the code to send an email becomes much cleaner. We will use asyncio.to_thread to ensure the HTTP request to Resend doesn&#39;t block your FastAPI event loop.

Update your app/services/notification_service.py:

Python
import os
import resend
import asyncio
from sqlalchemy.orm import Session
from app.models.notification import Notification

# Initialize Resend API Key
resend.api_key = os.getenv(&quot;RESEND_API_KEY&quot;)

def _send_resend_email(to_email: str, subject: str, html_body: str):
    &quot;&quot;&quot;
    Synchronous wrapper for the Resend SDK.
    &quot;&quot;&quot;
    params = {
        &quot;from&quot;: os.getenv(&quot;MAIL_FROM&quot;, &quot;onboarding@resend.dev&quot;),
        &quot;to&quot;: [to_email],
        &quot;subject&quot;: subject,
        &quot;html&quot;: html_body,
    }
    
    try:
        response = resend.Emails.send(params)
        print(f&quot;Successfully sent email to {to_email}. Resend ID: {response[&#39;id&#39;]}&quot;)
    except Exception as e:
        print(f&quot;Failed to send email to {to_email}. Error: {str(e)}&quot;)

def create_notification(db: Session, recipient_id: int, recipient_email: str, notif_type: str, title: str, message: str):
    &quot;&quot;&quot;
    Creates the DB record AND fires off the Resend email asynchronously.
    &quot;&quot;&quot;
    # 1. Save to Database (In-App Notification)
    db_notif = Notification(
        recipient_id=recipient_id,
        notification_type=notif_type,
        title=title,
        message=message
    )
    db.add(db_notif)
    db.commit()
    db.refresh(db_notif)

    # 2. Prepare the HTML payload
    email_body = f&quot;&quot;&quot;
    &lt;div style=&quot;font-family: sans-serif; max-width: 600px; margin: auto;&quot;&gt;
        &lt;h2 style=&quot;color: #2563eb;&quot;&gt;{title}&lt;/h2&gt;
        &lt;p style=&quot;color: #374151; font-size: 16px;&quot;&gt;{message}&lt;/p&gt;
        &lt;hr style=&quot;border: 1px solid #e5e7eb; margin-top: 30px;&quot;&gt;
        &lt;p style=&quot;color: #9ca3af; font-size: 12px;&quot;&gt;This is an automated notification from the PMS Platform.&lt;/p&gt;
    &lt;/div&gt;
    &quot;&quot;&quot;
    
    # 3. Send via Resend in a background thread to prevent blocking the API
    asyncio.create_task(asyncio.to_thread(_send_resend_email, recipient_email, title, email_body))

    return db_notif
Why this is the &quot;Pro&quot; move:
Speed: By using asyncio.to_thread, your API will respond to the frontend in milliseconds while Resend handles the heavy lifting in the background.

Deliverability: Resend&#39;s default onboarding@resend.dev domain bypasses spam filters automatically for testing, meaning your test emails won&#39;t end up in your spam folder like generic SMTP tests often do.

Tracking: If you log into your Resend dashboard, you will get a beautiful UI showing exactly who opened the email, if it bounced, or if a link was clicked.

If you want to implement this, just create an account at resend.com, generate an API key, and swap out the code above!

Based on the PRD, notifications serve specific purposes: to keep users informed, to prompt action, and to escalate when deadlines are missed. Below is a summary of all notification triggers mandated by the PRD, grouped by module, with the purpose and recipients.

1. Probation Module
Event	Purpose	Recipient(s)
Day 30 / 60 / 80 trigger fired	Notify that feedback forms are ready.	Employee &amp; Manager
Reminder after 2, 4, 6 days	Nudge submission before escalation.	Employee or Manager (whichever hasn’t submitted)
Escalation after 7 days	Alert Admin to intervene.	Admin
Probation paused / resumed	Inform of timeline changes (due to leave).	Employee &amp; Manager
Probation completed / rejected	Communicate final outcome.	Employee &amp; Manager
Why: Automate probation tracking, reduce manual follow‑up, and ensure timely confirmation decisions.

2. Performance Review Cycles
Event	Purpose	Recipient(s)
Cycle triggered	Announce start of review period with links to forms.	All eligible employees &amp; managers
Reminder on day 5 (gentle), day 15 (urgent)	Encourage submission before deadline.	Employee (self‑review) or Manager (manager feedback)
Escalation on day 22	Flag overdue forms to Admin.	Admin
Cycle closed	Notify that results are finalised.	All participants
Why: Drive completion rates &gt;85% without manual chasing, and escalate only when necessary.

3. Goal Management
Event	Purpose	Recipient(s)
Goal submitted for approval	Alert manager (or Admin if manager missing) to review.	Manager / Admin
Goal approved	Inform employee that goal is active.	Employee
Goal rejected (with reason)	Allow employee to revise and resubmit.	Employee
Goal pending approval &gt;5 business days	Escalate to Admin to prevent delays.	Admin
Why: Meet the PRD target of &lt;5 business days approval turnaround.

4. Feedback &amp; Flags
Event	Purpose	Recipient(s)
Red flag detected (score ≤2 or blank comment)	Alert Admin to review concerning feedback early.	Admin
Flag unresolved for 7 days	Ensure flags don’t get ignored.	Secondary Admin / escalation contact
Repeat flag pattern (employee flagged in 2+ consecutive cycles)	Highlight chronic issues for coaching.	Admin
Why: Enable proactive performance management and pattern detection as per PRD.

5. System / Admin Edge Cases
Event	Purpose	Recipient(s)
No manager assigned to employee (when probation trigger attempts)	Prompt Admin to assign a manager.	Admin
Manager changes mid‑cycle	Inform new manager of pending tasks; optionally inform old manager of handover.	New manager (and optionally old)
Admin offboarding	Transfer open flags and subscriptions to successor.	Designated successor Admin
Why: Handle edge cases defined in the PRD to maintain system integrity.

Email Content (as per PRD)
Probation emails include direct links to the feedback form.

Review cycle emails provide deadlines and a link to the review dashboard.

Goal approval emails contain the goal title, a comment (if rejected), and a link to view/edit.

Flag alerts summarise the concerning feedback and link to the admin review queue.

All notifications are also stored as in‑app notifications for easy access.

These triggers are already implemented in the backend via the NotificationService, and with your new email sender, they will now send real emails. The test script you created will verify the goal‑submission notification, which should reach the manager’s inbox.

Based on the Product Requirements Document (PRD) you shared earlier, the platform relies heavily on automated notifications to keep performance reviews and goal tracking on schedule without manual HR intervention.

Here is the exact breakdown of every scenario where an email notification must be sent, grouped by module:

1. Probation Module (New Hires)
These run on a strict working-day clock starting from the Date of Joining (DOJ).

Initial Triggers (Day 30 / 60 / 80): Sent to both the Employee and Manager containing links to their respective feedback forms.

Reminder Nudges (+2, +4, +6 Days): Sent to whoever has not submitted their form yet.

Escalation Alert (+7 Days): Sent to the Admin if forms are still pending, warning them of a compliance risk.

Missing Manager Alert: Sent to the Admin if a probation trigger is scheduled to fire but the employee has no assigned manager in the system.

2. Performance Review Cycles
These fire based on fixed calendar dates for Bi-Annual or Quarterly tracks.

Cycle Launch: Sent to the Employee and Manager on the 1st of the trigger month (e.g., Aug 1, Feb 1, Apr 1), prompting the employee to submit their self-rating.

Gentle Reminder (5th of the month): Sent to users with pending forms.

Urgent Reminder (15th of the month): Sent to users with pending forms.

Admin Escalation (22nd of the month): Sent to the Admin, providing a list of users who have ignored the reminders.

Missing Goals Alert: Sent to the Manager if cycle launch occurs but an employee has no active goals in the GMS, blocking their review.

3. Goal Management System (GMS)
These are event-driven, firing immediately when a user takes an action.

Goal Submitted for Approval: Sent to the Manager, requesting them to review and set weightage for a newly proposed employee goal.

Goal Approved: Sent to the Employee, confirming the goal is now active.

Goal Rejected: Sent to the Employee, containing the manager&#39;s reason/comment and prompting them to edit and resubmit.

Approval SLA Escalation: Sent to the Admin if a manager fails to approve/reject an employee&#39;s goal within 5 business days.

Company Goal Cascade Alert: Sent to Team/Individual Owners if an Admin changes a top-level Company Goal mid-cycle, giving them 5 days to acknowledge the change before escalating to the Admin.

4. Admin &amp; Risk Exceptions (Feedback &amp; Flags)
These are critical alerts routed primarily to HR or Leadership.

Orphaned Flag Alert: Sent to a Secondary Admin Contact if a flagged feedback response (low score or negative sentiment) sits in the primary Admin&#39;s queue for 7 days without action.

Admin Offboarding Transfer: Sent to a Successor when an Admin account is deactivated, prompting them to confirm ownership of pending flags and escalations.

Since you are currently wiring up the Resend / SMTP integration, you will need to map these exact scenarios to your notification_type database column.

## Key Insights

- **ARCHITECTURE**: PMS uses FastAPI + PostgreSQL + SQLAlchemy 2.0 + Alembic + APScheduler. Backend runs on port 8003, frontend on 3001. Docker service names must use hyphens not underscores for DNS resolution.

- **EMAIL SYSTEM**: Migrated from SMTP to Resend API for better performance and deliverability. Uses threading.Thread for background email sending to avoid blocking API responses and prevent "no running event loop" errors in FastAPI.

- **NOTIFICATION TRIGGERS**: All 15+ notification types implemented per PRD: Probation (Day 30/60/80 triggers, reminders at +2/+4/+6 days, escalation at +7 days), Review cycles (start, day 5/15 reminders, day 22 escalation), Goals (submitted, approved, rejected, >5 day escalation), Red flags (detected, unresolved after 7 days, repeat patterns), System alerts (missing manager, admin changes).

- **RESEND CONFIGURATION**: API key re_F5kbfcXY_48SWoSWe5j9sZFf1gULj7nHu configured. Emails sent from "PMS Platform <onboarding@resend.dev>". HTML templates use purple gradient header (#667eea to #764ba2) with professional styling.

- **WEIGHTAGE SYSTEM**: Goals use priority-based auto-weightage (CRITICAL=40%, HIGH=30%, MEDIUM=20%, LOW=10%). Total per user per tag period cannot exceed 100%. Test script uses weekly tag with low priority to avoid conflicts.

#!/usr/bin/env python3
&quot;&quot;&quot;
Test all notification triggers in PMS.
Make sure the backend is running and .env contains email config.
&quot;&quot;&quot;

import requests
import json
import time
from datetime import datetime, timedelta

BASE_URL = &quot;http://localhost:8003/api/v1&quot;

# Test user details
ADMIN_EMAIL = &quot;prashant.sharma@opstree.com&quot;
ADMIN_PASSWORD = &quot;admin123&quot;  # default, adjust if needed
MANAGER_EMAIL = &quot;gourav.singh@opstree.com&quot;
MANAGER_PASSWORD = &quot;manager123&quot;
EMPLOYEE_EMAIL = &quot;harshit.verma@opstree.com&quot;
EMPLOYEE_PASSWORD = &quot;employee123&quot;

# Default admin credentials for login (use the one that exists, e.g., sandeep)
DEFAULT_ADMIN_EMAIL = &quot;sandeep@opstree.com&quot;
DEFAULT_ADMIN_PASSWORD = &quot;admin123&quot;

# Store tokens
tokens = {}

def login(email, password):
    &quot;&quot;&quot;Login and return token.&quot;&quot;&quot;
    resp = requests.post(f&quot;{BASE_URL}/auth/login&quot;, json={&quot;email&quot;: email, &quot;password&quot;: password})
    if resp.status_code == 200:
        token = resp.json()[&quot;access_token&quot;]
        print(f&quot;✅ Logged in as {email}&quot;)
        return token
    else:
        print(f&quot;❌ Login failed for {email}: {resp.text}&quot;)
        return None

def create_user(token, email, name, role, manager_id=None, team_id=1):
    &quot;&quot;&quot;Create a user via API.&quot;&quot;&quot;
    headers = {&quot;Authorization&quot;: f&quot;Bearer {token}&quot;}
    data = {
        &quot;email&quot;: email,
        &quot;name&quot;: name,
        &quot;role&quot;: role,
        &quot;password&quot;: &quot;Test@123&quot;,  # set a default
        &quot;manager_id&quot;: manager_id,
        &quot;team_id&quot;: team_id,
        &quot;date_of_joining&quot;: datetime.now().strftime(&quot;%Y-%m-%d&quot;),
        &quot;is_active&quot;: True
    }
    resp = requests.post(f&quot;{BASE_URL}/users/&quot;, json=data, headers=headers)
    if resp.status_code == 201:
        print(f&quot;✅ Created user {email} (role={role})&quot;)
        return resp.json()[&quot;id&quot;]
    else:
        print(f&quot;❌ Failed to create user {email}: {resp.text}&quot;)
        return None

def get_user_id_by_email(token, email):
    &quot;&quot;&quot;Get user ID by email (list users).&quot;&quot;&quot;
    headers = {&quot;Authorization&quot;: f&quot;Bearer {token}&quot;}
    resp = requests.get(f&quot;{BASE_URL}/users/&quot;, headers=headers)
    if resp.status_code == 200:
        users = resp.json()
        for user in users:
            if user[&quot;email&quot;] == email:
                return user[&quot;id&quot;]
    return None

def create_goal(token, user_id, title, description, weightage=20, level=&quot;INDIVIDUAL&quot;, parent_id=None):
    &quot;&quot;&quot;Create a goal.&quot;&quot;&quot;
    headers = {&quot;Authorization&quot;: f&quot;Bearer {token}&quot;}
    data = {
        &quot;title&quot;: title,
        &quot;description&quot;: description,
        &quot;level&quot;: level,
        &quot;owner_id&quot;: user_id,
        &quot;weightage&quot;: weightage,
        &quot;parent_id&quot;: parent_id,
        &quot;status&quot;: &quot;DRAFT&quot;
    }
    resp = requests.post(f&quot;{BASE_URL}/goals/&quot;, json=data, headers=headers)
    if resp.status_code == 201:
        goal = resp.json()
        print(f&quot;✅ Created goal {goal[&#39;id&#39;]} for user {user_id}&quot;)
        return goal[&quot;id&quot;]
    else:
        print(f&quot;❌ Failed to create goal: {resp.text}&quot;)
        return None

def submit_goal(token, goal_id):
    &quot;&quot;&quot;Submit goal for approval.&quot;&quot;&quot;
    headers = {&quot;Authorization&quot;: f&quot;Bearer {token}&quot;}
    resp = requests.post(f&quot;{BASE_URL}/goals/{goal_id}/submit&quot;, headers=headers)
    if resp.status_code == 200:
        print(f&quot;✅ Submitted goal {goal_id}&quot;)
        return True
    else:
        print(f&quot;❌ Failed to submit goal: {resp.text}&quot;)
        return False

def approve_goal(token, goal_id):
    &quot;&quot;&quot;Approve goal (as manager).&quot;&quot;&quot;
    headers = {&quot;Authorization&quot;: f&quot;Bearer {token}&quot;}
    resp = requests.post(f&quot;{BASE_URL}/goals/{goal_id}/approve&quot;, headers=headers)
    if resp.status_code == 200:
        print(f&quot;✅ Approved goal {goal_id}&quot;)
        return True
    else:
        print(f&quot;❌ Failed to approve goal: {resp.text}&quot;)
        return False

def create_review_cycle(token, name, cycle_type, start_date, end_date):
    &quot;&quot;&quot;Create a review cycle.&quot;&quot;&quot;
    headers = {&quot;Authorization&quot;: f&quot;Bearer {token}&quot;}
    data = {
        &quot;cycle_name&quot;: name,
        &quot;cycle_type&quot;: cycle_type,
        &quot;start_date&quot;: start_date,
        &quot;end_date&quot;: end_date,
        &quot;self_review_deadline&quot;: end_date,
        &quot;manager_review_deadline&quot;: end_date,
    }
    resp = requests.post(f&quot;{BASE_URL}/review-cycles/&quot;, json=data, headers=headers)
    if resp.status_code == 201:
        cycle = resp.json()
        print(f&quot;✅ Created review cycle {cycle[&#39;id&#39;]}&quot;)
        return cycle[&quot;id&quot;]
    else:
        print(f&quot;❌ Failed to create cycle: {resp.text}&quot;)
        return None

def trigger_review_cycle(token, cycle_id):
    &quot;&quot;&quot;Trigger review cycle.&quot;&quot;&quot;
    headers = {&quot;Authorization&quot;: f&quot;Bearer {token}&quot;}
    resp = requests.post(f&quot;{BASE_URL}/review-cycles/{cycle_id}/trigger&quot;, headers=headers)
    if resp.status_code == 200:
        print(f&quot;✅ Triggered review cycle {cycle_id}&quot;)
        return True
    else:
        print(f&quot;❌ Failed to trigger cycle: {resp.text}&quot;)
        return False

def submit_review_form(token, form_id, form_data):
    &quot;&quot;&quot;Submit a review form.&quot;&quot;&quot;
    headers = {&quot;Authorization&quot;: f&quot;Bearer {token}&quot;}
    data = {
        &quot;form_data&quot;: form_data,
        &quot;final_rating&quot;: 2  # low rating to trigger flag
    }
    resp = requests.post(f&quot;{BASE_URL}/review-forms/{form_id}/submit&quot;, json=data, headers=headers)
    if resp.status_code == 200:
        print(f&quot;✅ Submitted review form {form_id}&quot;)
        return True
    else:
        print(f&quot;❌ Failed to submit review form: {resp.text}&quot;)
        return False

def get_my_review_forms(token):
    &quot;&quot;&quot;Get current user&#39;s review forms.&quot;&quot;&quot;
    headers = {&quot;Authorization&quot;: f&quot;Bearer {token}&quot;}
    resp = requests.get(f&quot;{BASE_URL}/review-forms/&quot;, headers=headers)
    if resp.status_code == 200:
        return resp.json()
    else:
        print(f&quot;❌ Failed to get forms: {resp.text}&quot;)
        return []

def check_flags(token):
    &quot;&quot;&quot;Check admin flags.&quot;&quot;&quot;
    headers = {&quot;Authorization&quot;: f&quot;Bearer {token}&quot;}
    resp = requests.get(f&quot;{BASE_URL}/admin/flags&quot;, headers=headers)
    if resp.status_code == 200:
        flags = resp.json()
        print(f&quot;📋 Found {len(flags)} flags.&quot;)
        return flags
    else:
        print(f&quot;❌ Failed to get flags: {resp.text}&quot;)
        return []

def main():
    # 1. Login as default admin to create users if needed
    admin_token = login(DEFAULT_ADMIN_EMAIL, DEFAULT_ADMIN_PASSWORD)
    if not admin_token:
        print(&quot;Cannot proceed without admin token.&quot;)
        return

    # 2. Get or create users
    admin_id = get_user_id_by_email(admin_token, ADMIN_EMAIL)
    manager_id = get_user_id_by_email(admin_token, MANAGER_EMAIL)
    employee_id = get_user_id_by_email(admin_token, EMPLOYEE_EMAIL)

    if not admin_id:
        print(f&quot;Creating admin {ADMIN_EMAIL}&quot;)
        admin_id = create_user(admin_token, ADMIN_EMAIL, &quot;Prashant Sharma&quot;, &quot;admin&quot;)
    if not manager_id:
        print(f&quot;Creating manager {MANAGER_EMAIL}&quot;)
        manager_id = create_user(admin_token, MANAGER_EMAIL, &quot;Gourav Singh&quot;, &quot;manager&quot;, manager_id=admin_id)
    if not employee_id:
        print(f&quot;Creating employee {EMPLOYEE_EMAIL}&quot;)
        employee_id = create_user(admin_token, EMPLOYEE_EMAIL, &quot;Harshit Verma&quot;, &quot;member&quot;, manager_id=manager_id)

    # 3. Login as employee to create and submit goal
    emp_token = login(EMPLOYEE_EMAIL, &quot;Test@123&quot;)  # use password we set
    if not emp_token:
        print(&quot;Employee login failed, maybe password mismatch. Attempting to set password?&quot;)
        # Could update password via admin, but skip for now.
        emp_token = login(EMPLOYEE_EMAIL, EMPLOYEE_PASSWORD)  # try default
        if not emp_token:
            print(&quot;Employee login failed. Exiting.&quot;)
            return

    # 4. Create a goal for employee
    goal_id = create_goal(emp_token, employee_id, &quot;Test Goal&quot;, &quot;This is a test goal for notification&quot;)
    if not goal_id:
        print(&quot;Goal creation failed. Exiting.&quot;)
        return

    # 5. Submit goal for approval (should email manager)
    submit_goal(emp_token, goal_id)
    time.sleep(2)  # give email time to send

    # 6. Login as manager and approve goal (should email employee)
    mgr_token = login(MANAGER_EMAIL, &quot;Test@123&quot;)
    if not mgr_token:
        print(&quot;Manager login failed. Exiting.&quot;)
        return
    approve_goal(mgr_token, goal_id)
    time.sleep(2)

    # 7. Create and trigger a review cycle
    today = datetime.now().strftime(&quot;%Y-%m-%d&quot;)
    future = (datetime.now() + timedelta(days=30)).strftime(&quot;%Y-%m-%d&quot;)
    cycle_id = create_review_cycle(admin_token, &quot;Test Cycle&quot;, &quot;QUARTERLY&quot;, today, future)
    if cycle_id:
        trigger_review_cycle(admin_token, cycle_id)
        time.sleep(2)  # let emails send

        # 8. Login as employee to submit self-assessment with low rating
        forms = get_my_review_forms(emp_token)
        for form in forms:
            if form[&quot;form_type&quot;] == &quot;SELF_ASSESSMENT&quot; and form[&quot;status&quot;] == &quot;PENDING&quot;:
                form_data = {
                    &quot;achievements&quot;: &quot;Some work done&quot;,
                    &quot;challenges&quot;: &quot;Some challenges&quot;,
                    &quot;rating&quot;: 1  # low rating triggers flag
                }
                submit_review_form(emp_token, form[&quot;id&quot;], form_data)
                break
        time.sleep(2)

    # 9. Check flags as admin (should have a flag for low rating)
    flags = check_flags(admin_token)
    if flags:
        print(&quot;✅ Red flag detected and email should have been sent to admin.&quot;)
    else:
        print(&quot;⚠️ No flags found. Check if flagging logic is triggered.&quot;)

    print(&quot;\n✅ Test completed. Check email inboxes for notifications.&quot;)
    print(&quot;  - Goal submission: manager (gourav.singh@opstree.com)&quot;)
    print(&quot;  - Goal approval: employee (harshit.verma@opstree.com)&quot;)
    print(&quot;  - Review cycle triggered: all eligible employees (including employee)&quot;)
    print(&quot;  - Red flag detected: admin (prashant.sharma@opstree.com)&quot;)

if __name__ == &quot;__main__&quot;:
    main()

This will confirm whether emails were actually sent and if any errors occurred.

Troubleshooting
Email not sent: Check that EMAIL_ENABLED=true and the backend is using the correct email backend (Resend/SMTP). Look for errors in the logs.

User creation fails: If the user already exists, the script will skip creation. Ensure the manager_id and team_id are valid.

Login fails: The default passwords might differ. You can adjust the script to use the actual passwords or use admin to reset them.

We need to answer: &quot;skip resend how we can use notificatio for mail&quot; – likely meaning &quot;skip Resend, how can we use notification for mail?&quot; i.e., alternative to Resend for email notifications.

Given previous context, the user wants to send email notifications without using Resend. Options: SMTP (e.g., Gmail, Outlook, custom SMTP), SendGrid, Mailgun, Amazon SES, or just keep the stub. Since we already have an email sender with pluggable backends (stub, smtp, sendgrid), we can configure SMTP.

We should provide instructions to set up SMTP (e.g., using Gmail app password) as previously done, or use SendGrid if preferred. The user earlier configured Gmail but had issues? They said &quot;skip resend&quot; so they don&#39;t want to use Resend. So answer with alternatives.

We&#39;ll explain how to use SMTP (with Gmail or any SMTP) as a fallback, including steps to set it up in .env, ensuring email backend is set to &quot;smtp&quot;. Also mention that if they want a free alternative, SendGrid offers 100 emails/day free tier. But SMTP is simplest.

We should also clarify that the notification service is already designed to send emails; they just need to configure the backend.

Let&#39;s craft a concise, helpful answer.

Using Email Notifications Without Resend
If you want to skip Resend, you have several alternative ways to send email notifications. Your current EmailSender supports SMTP and SendGrid out of the box. Choose one:

Option 1: SMTP (e.g., Gmail, Outlook, corporate SMTP)
This is the simplest – use your existing email account or a dedicated SMTP server.

Configure .env (example with Gmail):

env
EMAIL_ENABLED=true
EMAIL_BACKEND=smtp
SMTP_HOST=smtp.gmail.com
SMTP_PORT=587
SMTP_USER=your-email@gmail.com
SMTP_PASSWORD=your-app-password   # not your regular password
SMTP_USE_TLS=true
DEFAULT_FROM_EMAIL=&quot;PMS Platform &lt;your-email@gmail.com&gt;&quot;
If using Gmail, you must use an App Password (enable 2FA first). For other providers, use their SMTP credentials.

Restart backend:

bash
docker compose restart gms-backend
Option 2: SendGrid (Free tier: 100 emails/day)
SendGrid offers a generous free plan and is easy to integrate.

Sign up at sendgrid.com and get an API key.

Configure .env:

env
EMAIL_ENABLED=true
EMAIL_BACKEND=sendgrid
SENDGRID_API_KEY=your-sendgrid-api-key
DEFAULT_FROM_EMAIL=&quot;PMS Platform &lt;noreply@yourdomain.com&gt;&quot;
You need to verify a sender (single email or domain) in SendGrid.

Restart backend.

Option 3: Keep the Stub (Console Logging)
If you only need to see what emails would be sent (for testing), set:

env
EMAIL_ENABLED=true
EMAIL_BACKEND=stub
Emails will be printed to the console (docker logs). This is useful during development.

How to Verify It Works
Trigger any notification (e.g., create a goal and submit it).

Check the backend logs: docker logs pms-gms-backend-1 --tail 30.

Look for [EMAIL] lines – they indicate successful send attempts.

Also check the recipient’s inbox (and spam folder).

If you encounter SMTP authentication errors, double‑check the password (use an app password for Gmail) and ensure EMAIL_ENABLED=true is set.

Your notification service is already written to call the email sender for all PRD‑defined events – no code changes needed. Just pick the email backend that suits you best.

If we are skipping Resend, we will route the notifications directly through a standard SMTP server (like your company&#39;s Google Workspace, Microsoft 365, or a personal Gmail account with an App Password).

This is actually a very common approach for internal HR tools because it keeps all email traffic strictly within your company&#39;s existing infrastructure, and you don&#39;t have to pay a third-party API service.

Here is how to build a native, dependency-free email notification system using Python&#39;s built-in libraries. You do not even need to install fastapi-mail for this.

Step 1: Your .env Variables
Make sure your environment variables are configured for your SMTP provider (e.g., Gmail).

Code snippet
# backend/gms-backend/.env
SMTP_HOST=smtp.gmail.com
SMTP_PORT=587
SMTP_USER=harshit.verma@opstree.com
SMTP_PASSWORD=your_16_character_app_password
Step 2: The Native SMTP Utility
Create a new file: app/utils/email_sender.py.
This script uses Python&#39;s built-in smtplib and email modules to securely construct and send an HTML email.

Python
import os
import smtplib
from email.mime.text import MIMEText
from email.mime.multipart import MIMEMultipart

def send_smtp_email_sync(to_email: str, subject: str, html_body: str):
    &quot;&quot;&quot;
    Constructs an HTML email and sends it via standard SMTP.
    &quot;&quot;&quot;
    smtp_host = os.getenv(&quot;SMTP_HOST&quot;, &quot;smtp.gmail.com&quot;)
    smtp_port = int(os.getenv(&quot;SMTP_PORT&quot;, 587))
    smtp_user = os.getenv(&quot;SMTP_USER&quot;)
    smtp_password = os.getenv(&quot;SMTP_PASSWORD&quot;)

    # 1. Construct the Email Message
    msg = MIMEMultipart(&quot;alternative&quot;)
    msg[&quot;Subject&quot;] = subject
    msg[&quot;From&quot;] = f&quot;PMS Notifications &lt;{smtp_user}&gt;&quot;
    msg[&quot;To&quot;] = to_email

    # Attach the HTML body
    html_part = MIMEText(html_body, &quot;html&quot;)
    msg.attach(html_part)

    # 2. Connect to the Server and Send
    try:
        # Establish a secure session with the SMTP server
        with smtplib.SMTP(smtp_host, smtp_port) as server:
            server.starttls() # Secure the connection
            server.login(smtp_user, smtp_password)
            server.sendmail(smtp_user, to_email, msg.as_string())
            
        print(f&quot;[SMTP] Successfully sent email to {to_email}&quot;)
    except Exception as e:
        print(f&quot;[SMTP] Error sending email to {to_email}: {str(e)}&quot;)
Step 3: Wire it into the Notification Service
We update your notification_service.py to use this new native sender. We will keep the get_email_template function (the HTML formatter we created earlier) and use asyncio.to_thread so the slow SMTP handshake doesn&#39;t freeze your FastAPI application.

Python
import asyncio
from sqlalchemy.orm import Session
from app.models.notification import Notification
from app.utils.email_templates import get_email_template
from app.utils.email_sender import send_smtp_email_sync

def create_notification(db: Session, recipient_id: int, recipient_email: str, notif_type: str, title: str, message: str):
    &quot;&quot;&quot;
    Creates the DB record AND fires off the SMTP email asynchronously.
    &quot;&quot;&quot;
    # 1. Save to Database (Frontend In-App Notification)
    db_notif = Notification(
        recipient_id=recipient_id,
        notification_type=notif_type,
        title=title,
        message=message
    )
    db.add(db_notif)
    db.commit()
    db.refresh(db_notif)

    # 2. Generate the Branded HTML
    html_content = get_email_template(notif_type, title, message)
    
    # 3. Send via SMTP in a background thread
    asyncio.create_task(
        asyncio.to_thread(send_smtp_email_sync, recipient_email, title, html_content)
    )

    return db_notif
How this works in practice:
Whenever any part of your system calls create_notification, two things happen simultaneously:

The notification is instantly saved to the PostgreSQL database so the employee sees a red badge on the bell icon in their dashboard.

A background thread silently logs into your Gmail/Opstree SMTP server, attaches the beautiful HTML template, and delivers it to the user&#39;s actual email inbox.

import os

from email.mime.text import MIMEText
from email.mime.multipart import MIMEMultipart
from dotenv import load_dotenv

# Load SMTP credentials from .env
load_dotenv()
SMTP_HOST = os.getenv(&quot;SMTP_HOST&quot;, &quot;smtp.gmail.com&quot;)
SMTP_PORT = int(os.getenv(&quot;SMTP_PORT&quot;, 587))
SMTP_USER = os.getenv(&quot;SMTP_USER&quot;)
SMTP_PASSWORD = os.getenv(&quot;SMTP_PASSWORD&quot;)

# === THE USERS ===
EMP_EMAIL = &quot;vermajiharshit1@gmail.com&quot;  # Employee: Harshit
MGR_EMAIL = &quot;gourav95411@gmail.com&quot;      # Manager: Gourav
ADMIN_EMAIL = &quot;gourav95411@gmail.com&quot;    # Admin: Routed to Gourav for testing

# === THE HTML TEMPLATE GENERATOR ===
def get_email_template(notif_type: str, title: str, message: str) -&gt; str:
    if notif_type in [&quot;goal_submitted&quot;, &quot;review_cycle_started&quot;]:
        color = &quot;#3b82f6&quot; # Blue
        icon = &quot;🎯&quot;
    elif notif_type == &quot;goal_approved&quot;:
        color = &quot;#10b981&quot; # Green
        icon = &quot;✅&quot;
    elif notif_type.startswith(&quot;probation_trigger&quot;):
        color = &quot;#8b5cf6&quot; # Purple
        icon = &quot;📅&quot;
    elif notif_type in [&quot;probation_escalation&quot;, &quot;flag_alert&quot;]:
        color = &quot;#ef4444&quot; # Red
        icon = &quot;⚠️&quot;
    else:
        color = &quot;#6b7280&quot; # Gray
        icon = &quot;🔔&quot;

    return f&quot;&quot;&quot;
    &lt;div style=&quot;font-family: &#39;Segoe UI&#39;, Tahoma, sans-serif; max-width: 600px; margin: 0 auto; border: 1px solid #e5e7eb; border-radius: 8px; overflow: hidden;&quot;&gt;
        &lt;div style=&quot;background-color: {color}; padding: 20px; text-align: center;&quot;&gt;
            &lt;h2 style=&quot;color: #ffffff; margin: 0; font-size: 24px;&quot;&gt;{icon} PMS Notification&lt;/h2&gt;
        &lt;/div&gt;
        &lt;div style=&quot;padding: 30px;&quot;&gt;
            &lt;h3 style=&quot;color: #1f2937; margin-top: 0;&quot;&gt;{title}&lt;/h3&gt;
            &lt;p style=&quot;color: #4b5563; font-size: 16px; line-height: 1.5;&quot;&gt;{message}&lt;/p&gt;
            &lt;div style=&quot;margin-top: 30px; text-align: center;&quot;&gt;
                &lt;a href=&quot;http://localhost:3000&quot; style=&quot;background-color: {color}; color: #ffffff; padding: 12px 24px; text-decoration: none; border-radius: 6px; font-weight: bold; display: inline-block;&quot;&gt;Open PMS Dashboard&lt;/a&gt;
            &lt;/div&gt;
        &lt;/div&gt;
        &lt;div style=&quot;background-color: #f9fafb; padding: 15px; text-align: center; border-top: 1px solid #e5e7eb;&quot;&gt;
            &lt;p style=&quot;color: #9ca3af; font-size: 12px; margin: 0;&quot;&gt;Automated message from the Performance Management System.&lt;/p&gt;
        &lt;/div&gt;
    &lt;/div&gt;
    &quot;&quot;&quot;

def send_test_email(to_email: str, subject: str, html_body: str):
    msg = MIMEMultipart(&quot;alternative&quot;)
    msg[&quot;Subject&quot;] = subject
    msg[&quot;From&quot;] = f&quot;PMS Platform &lt;{SMTP_USER}&gt;&quot;
    msg[&quot;To&quot;] = to_email
    msg.attach(MIMEText(html_body, &quot;html&quot;))

    with smtplib.SMTP(SMTP_HOST, SMTP_PORT) as server:
        server.starttls()
        server.login(SMTP_USER, SMTP_PASSWORD)
        server.sendmail(SMTP_USER, to_email, msg.as_string())

# === THE PMS USE CASES ===
test_cases = [
    {
        &quot;to&quot;: MGR_EMAIL,
        &quot;subject&quot;: &quot;Action Required: New Goal Proposal&quot;,

we covered all the functionality for this project or not We need to transform the provided PRD into a structured implementation plan. Below is a comprehensive blueprint covering system architecture, data models, API endpoints, automation workflows, and a phased rollout strategy.

Implementation Plan: Performance Management System (PMS)


1. High-Level Architecture

Frontend: React/Vue.js dashboard with role-based views (Employee, Manager, Admin).

Backend: Node.js (Express) / Python (Django) – RESTful API, event-driven for triggers.

Database: PostgreSQL (relational for users, goals, forms) + Redis (caching, job queues).

Scheduler: Cron jobs / Celery beat for email triggers, reminders, and escalations.

Email Service: SendGrid / AWS SES with templated emails.

Audit Logging: All state changes logged in a dedicated audit table.


2. Core Data Models

User

id, name, email, role (employee, manager, admin), manager_id (nullable), doj (date), status (active, inactive, on_leave), leave_start, leave_end, created_at, updated_at.

Goal

id, user_id (owner), creator_id (who created), title, description, status (draft, pending_approval, active, completed, archived), weight (decimal), completion_percentage (integer), company_goal_id (nullable, for cascading), team_goal_id (nullable), created_at, approved_at, approved_by, rejection_reason.

GoalHierarchy

id, type (company, team, individual), parent_id (null for company), goal_id, order.

FeedbackForm

id, type (probation_day30, probation_day60, probation_day80, bi_annual, quarterly), user_id, manager_id, period_start, period_end, status (pending, submitted, cross_shared), submitted_at, manager_submitted_at, employee_submitted_at, scores (JSON), open_ended_responses (JSON), flags (JSON, e.g., red_flag, soft_flag), flag_reviewed_at, flag_reviewed_by.

ReviewCycle

id, name, type (bi_annual, quarterly), start_date, end_date, trigger_date, reminder_dates (JSON), status (active, closed), eligible_employee_ids (array).

Notification

id, user_id, type (email, in_app), subject, body, sent_at, read_at, status.

AuditLog

id, user_id, action, entity_type, entity_id, old_value, new_value, created_at.


3. API Endpoints (Key Ones) Endpoint	Method	Description	Roles /api/goals	GET	List goals (filter by user, status)	All /api/goals	POST	Create goal (draft)	All /api/goals/:id/submit	POST	Submit goal for approval	Employee /api/goals/:id/approve	POST	Approve goal	Manager, Admin /api/goals/:id/reject	POST	Reject goal with reason	Manager, Admin /api/goals/:id/update-progress	PATCH	Update completion %	Employee, Manager /api/feedback/forms	GET	List forms for user	All (filtered) /api/feedback/forms/:id/submit	POST	Submit self/manager feedback	Employee/Manager /api/feedback/flags	GET	List flagged feedback (admin only)	Admin /api/feedback/flags/:id/resolve	POST	Mark flag reviewed	Admin /api/cycles	GET	List review cycles	All /api/cycles/:id/trigger	POST	Manually trigger cycle emails	Admin /api/users/:id/pause-probation	POST	Pause probation due to leave	Admin /api/dashboard	GET	Role‑specific dashboard data	All
4. Automation Workflows

4\.1 Probation Triggers

Trigger: Daily cron at 00:00 UTC.

Find employees with doj + X working days = today (X = 30, 60, 80).

Exclude those on leave (probation paused).

Create FeedbackForm records for employee and manager.

Send email with unique form link.

Reminders: Scheduled at +2, +4, +6 working days if form not submitted.

Escalation: At +7 days, email Admin and mark as flag in dashboard.

4\.2 Review Cycle Triggers

Trigger: On cycle start date (e.g., Aug 1 for bi-annual).

Determine eligible employees (joined &gt;60 days before cycle close).

Create FeedbackForm for each employee–manager pair.

Reminders: Scheduled for cycle day 5, 15, 22 (calendar days) with increasing urgency.

Escalation: At day 22, Admin receives list of pending submissions.

4\.3 Goal Approval Escalation

When: Goal pending_approval older than 5 business days.

Action: Send email to Admin, mark as high‑priority in dashboard.

4\.4 Flag Auto‑tagging

When: Feedback form submitted.

Logic:

Red flag if any score ≤ threshold (default 2/5) OR negative sentiment in text (using simple keyword list or ML).

Soft flag if open‑ended fields left blank.

Result: Add entry to admin flag queue.

4\.5 Pattern Detection

When: New flag added.

Logic: Check historical flags for same employee across cycles. If flag count ≥ 2, attach “Repeat Flag” alert to the flag entry.


5. Database Considerations

Working Days Calculation: Store public holidays + weekends in a non_working_days table for accurate probation timers. Use a stored function or application‑side logic.

Concurrency: Use row‑level locks when updating goals (weightage, completion %) to avoid race conditions.

Soft Deletes: Goals and forms should have deleted_at for auditability.

Indexes: On user_id, status, submitted_at, flag_reviewed_at.


6. Role‑Based Access Control (RBAC) Employee: Read/write own goals (except approved goals); submit feedback; view own feedback after cross‑share.

Manager: All employee permissions for direct reports; approve/reject team goals; view team dashboards; submit manager feedback.

Admin: Full read/write; override approvals; manage users; configure thresholds; view all flags and reports.


7. Edge Case Implementation Details

Probation Pause: When user leave is recorded, set probation_paused_until; recalc trigger dates on return.

Manager Change: Reassign pending forms to new manager; old manager’s submissions remain as read‑only history.

No Manager Assigned: Admin alert on trigger attempt; admin can assign temporary manager or override.

Dual‑track Employees: Store a flag skip_bi_annual if quarterly cycle overlaps; during cycle creation, check overlaps and exclude.

Goal Ownership Transfer: When employee changes team, keep goal ownership; old manager retains read access; new manager gets edit.

Company Goal Updates: Use a suggestion pattern – create a notification for affected goal owners, allow 5 days to acknowledge; if no action, notify Admin.


8. Implementation Roadmap

Phase 0 – Foundation (Week 1–2) Set up project structure, authentication, RBAC.

User CRUD with manager hierarchy.

Basic goal CRUD (no approval flow yet).

Phase 1 – Probation &amp; Feedback (Week 3–4) Working days utility + probation trigger engine.

Feedback form templates (self/manager) with storage.

Cross‑share logic + basic submission UI.

Admin dashboard skeleton.

Phase 2 – Review Cycles &amp; Reminders (Week 5–6) Cycle definitions, eligibility calculation.

Email triggers for bi‑annual &amp; quarterly cycles.

Reminder scheduler + escalation engine.

Red‑flag auto‑tagging (configurable threshold).

Phase 3 – Goal Management (Week 7–8) Goal approval workflow (submit, approve, reject).

Weightage validation (must sum to 100%).

Cascading goals (company → team → individual) with roll‑up.

Completion % updates and aggregation.

Phase 4 – Admin &amp; Reporting (Week 9–10) Admin dashboard enhancements (flags, trends, export).

Pattern detection for repeat flags.

Audit logs for all state changes.

CSV/PDF export for feedback and goals.

Phase 5 – Polish &amp; Edge Cases (Week 11–12) Handle all edge cases (leave, manager change, dual‑track).

Performance optimization (indexes, caching).

User acceptance testing (UAT) with mock data.

Documentation &amp; deployment plan.


9. Success Metrics Monitoring

Probation triggers: Monitor via scheduled job logs; alert if any employee missed.

Completion rates: Daily dashboard showing % submitted per cycle.

Admin flag turnaround: Track time between flag creation and resolution.

Goal approval turnaround: Average time from submission to approval.

Implement a simple analytics dashboard for these KPIs.

This plan translates the PRD into actionable steps, ensuring all functional requirements, edge cases, and automation rules are addressed. Let me know if you need deeper dives into any module (e.g., exact cron expressions, database schemas, or frontend component breakdowns).
